Framed cohomological Hall algebras and cohomological stable envelopes
Authors: Tommaso Maria Botta
Published: 2023

ABSTRACT
There are multiple conjectures relating the cohomological Hall algebras (CoHAs) of certain substacks of the moduli stack of representations of a quiver Q to the Yangian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^{Q}_\textrm{MO}$$\end{document}YMOQ by Maulik–Okounkov, whose construction is based on the notion of stable envelopes of Nakajima varieties. In this article, we introduce the cohomological Hall algebra of the moduli stack of framed representations of a quiver Q (framed CoHA), and we show that the equivariant cohomology of the disjoint union of the Nakajima varieties \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}_Q(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}MQ(v,w) for all dimension vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {v}$$\end{document}v and framing vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {w}$$\end{document}w has a canonical structure of subalgebra of the framed CoHA. Restricted to this subalgebra, the algebra multiplication is identified with the stable envelope map. As a corollary, we deduce an explicit inductive formula to compute stable envelopes in terms of tautological classes.

Introduction

Overview

The purpose of this article is to establish a connection between the theory of stable envelopes and the world of cohomological Hall algebras.

The theory of stable envelopes, developed by Okounkov and its coauthors in the last decade [1, 19, 24–26], is the geometric answer to the problem of constructing a quantum group associated with an arbitrary quiver and of studying the monodromy of the associated quantum differential and difference equations. In a nutshell, the stable envelopes of a sufficiently nice variety X equipped with a torus action are certain axiomatically defined correspondences in cohomology that encode the equivariant geometry of X. Stable envelopes can be defined for many different varieties X. Still, the case of Nakajima varieties is particularly relevant. Indeed, not only are the latter one of the richest sources of symplectic resolutions, for which stable envelopes enjoy nicer properties, but they also bridge the gap between the abstract theory of stable envelopes and the Lie-theoretic realm of quantum groups. This follows from the fact that a choice of Nakajima variety is, first and foremost, a choice of a quiver, and its associated stable envelopes give rise to R-matrices for the corresponding quantum group.

The second fundamental ingredient for this article is the theory of cohomological Hall algebras (CoHAs). In the literature, many different constructions go under the name of CoHAs. Their common feature is being certain associative algebras whose underlying vector space encodes irreducible representations of a quiver and whose multiplication encodes extensions of representations. The adjective “cohomological” distinguishes them from the previous notion of Hall algebras [33], highlighting that their construction depends on the choice of a generalized cohomology theory. Schiffmann and Vasserot introduced the first example of CoHA for the Jordan quiver [10]. Since then, the theory of CoHAs has rapidly developed and proved influential in many areas of mathematics. There are two main approaches to CoHAs. The first one, initiated by Kontsevich and Soibelman [18] and often referred to as 3d CoHAs, is deeply connected with Donaldson–Thomas theory and BPS invariants. The second one, known as 2d CoHAs, is related to the first via dimensional reduction [9] but can be more directly related to quantum groups [34, 35, 41].

In this article, we introduce a variant of Kontsevich–Soibelman’s CoHA that involves moduli stacks of framed representations, and we argue that this algebra governs the stable envelopes of Nakajima varieties. More precisely, we realize the cohomology of the disjoint union of all possible Nakajima varieties associated with a quiver Q as a subalgebra of the framed CoHA, and we show that on this subalgebra CoHA’s multiplication reduces to stable envelopes. As an application, we exploit the shuffle description of the CoHA product to derive an inductive formula producing tautological presentations of the stable envelopes.

The general philosophy of the geometric representation theory of CoHAs is that taking framings corresponds to studying modules over the (unframed) CoHA. As a consequence, one can interpret this article’s result as evidence of the fact that the modules themselves, considered altogether, admit an algebra structure, which we call framed CoHA and that this algebra contains the algebra of stable envelopes of Nakajima varieties.

Framed cohomological Hall algebras

The acronym CoHA hints at a large class of algebras constructed from quiver representations and a given cohomology theory, possibly with some extra data such as some potential. Stable envelopes of Nakajima varieties

An important class of symplectic varieties associated with framed representations of a quiver is given by Nakajima quiver varieties [14, 21–23]. R-matrices and Yangians

Stable envelopes of Nakajima varieties give rise to R-matrices [19, Chapter 4]. We now briefly review their construction. Taking direct sums of (3) with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {v}_1$$\end{document}v1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {v}_2$$\end{document}v2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {v}$$\end{document}v, one gets a map (which we denote in the same way)4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Stab}_{{\mathfrak {C}}}:H_{T_{\text {w}_1}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {w}_1))\otimes _\Bbbk H_{T_{\text {w}_2}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {w}_2))\rightarrow H_{T_{\text {w}_1+\text {w}_2}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {w}_1+\text {w}_2)) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}StabC:HTw1(M(w1))⊗kHTw2(M(w2))→HTw1+w2(M(w1+w2))where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}(\text {w}_i)=\bigsqcup _{\text {v}_i} {\mathcal {M}}(\text {v}_i,\text {w}_i)$$\end{document}M(wi)=⨆viM(vi,wi). Given a pair of different chambers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C_1}$$\end{document}C1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C_2}$$\end{document}C2, the composition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R_{\mathfrak {C_1}, \mathfrak {C_2}}:=\text {Stab}_{\mathfrak {C_1}}^{-1}\circ \text {Stab}_{\mathfrak {C_2}}\in \text {End}(H_{T_{\text {w}_1}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {w}_1))_{loc}\otimes _\Bbbk H_{T_{\text {w}_2}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {w}_2))_{loc}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}RC1,C2:=StabC1-1∘StabC2∈End(HTw1(M(w1))loc⊗kHTw2(M(w2))loc)is called geometric R-matrix. Here, the subscript loc stands for localization with respect to the equivariant parameters coming from the framing. In other words, we set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{T_{\text {w}}}(-)_{loc}=H_{T_{\text {w}}}(-)\otimes _{\Bbbk }\text {Frac}(H_{A_{\text {w}}}(*)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HTw(-)loc=HTw(-)⊗kFrac(HAw(∗)).Localization is required to define the inverse \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \text {Stab}_{\mathfrak {C_1}}^{-1}$$\end{document}StabC1-1 of a stable envelope.

A foundational result of Maulik–Okounkov’s theory is that these R-matrices satisfy the Yang–Baxter equation with spectral parameter. The associated quantum group, defined via RTT relations, is the Maulik–Okounkov’s Yangian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^Q_\textrm{MO}$$\end{document}YMOQ associated with the quiver Q [19, Chapter 5]. The subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^Q_\textrm{MO}$$\end{document}YMOQ spanned by the matrix elements of the classical r-matrix r, defined as the equivariant residue of\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R_{\mathfrak {C_1}, \mathfrak {C_2}}(a)=1+ \frac{\hbar }{a} r+ O(a^{-2}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}RC1,C2(a)=1+ħar+O(a-2)is the Maulik–Okounkov’s Lie algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {g}}_Q$$\end{document}gQ.

Framed preprojective semistable CoHA

The apparent similarity with (2) suggests tentatively defining an algebra structure on\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}=\bigoplus _{\text {v},\text {w}\in \mathbb {N}^I}\mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}(\text {v},\text {w}) \qquad \qquad \mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}(\text {v},\text {w}):=H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr=⨁v,w∈NIHQ¯fr(v,w)HQ¯fr(v,w):=HTw(M(v,w))via the stable envelopes maps (3). Indeed, this construction defines a graded associative algebra structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr, which we call the framed semistable preprojective cohomological Hall algebra of Q. The choice of the name can be partially understood by observing that the vector space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr consists of cohomologies of Nakajima varieties, which are exactly moduli spaces of semistable representations of the preprojective path algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}{\overline{Q}}_{\text {fr}}/ J_\mu $$\end{document}CQ¯fr/Jμ of the doubled framed quiver \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{Q}}_{\text {fr}}$$\end{document}Q¯fr, but of course, more is needed to justify this definition. Namely, one must show that the multiplication defined by stable envelopes has something to do with CoHA’s multiplication. This is the main goal of the article. To accomplish this, we exploit Aganagic and Okounkov’s non-abelian stable envelope [2], a map of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ H_{T_\text {w}}(*)$$\end{document}HTw(∗)-modules5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{\psi }: H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w}))\rightarrow H_{T_\text {w}}([T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/G_\text {v}]) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ:HTw(M(v,w))→HTw([T∗Rep(v,w)/Gv])that solves, in analogy with the ordinary stable envelopes, an interpolation problem for cohomology classes [26]. In a nutshell, the assignment \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \mapsto \varvec{\psi }(\alpha )$$\end{document}α↦ψ(α) can be thought of as a distinguished extension of the cohomology class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}M(v,w) to the whole stack of representations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/ G_{\text {v}}]$$\end{document}[T∗Rep(v,w)/Gv]. The extension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }(\alpha )$$\end{document}ψ(α) is characterized by a precise support condition, namely being supported on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mu _{\text {v},\text {w}}^{-1}(0)/G_{\text {v}}]\subset [T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/ G_{\text {v}}]$$\end{document}[μv,w-1(0)/Gv]⊂[T∗Rep(v,w)/Gv]. As a result, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ is, up to a multiplicative factor, a section of the tautological pullback\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{j}^*: H_{T_\text {w}}([T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/ G_{\text {v}}])\twoheadrightarrow H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}j∗:HTw([T∗Rep(v,w)/Gv])↠HTw(M(v,w))associated with the inclusion\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{j}:{\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})= \mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}}(0)^{ss}/G_{\text {v}}\hookrightarrow [T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/ G_{\text {v}}]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}j:M(v,w)=μv,w-1(0)ss/Gv↪[T∗Rep(v,w)/Gv].With this background, we can finally compare the algebra structures on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr. Firstly, consider the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }: \mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}\rightarrow {\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}ψ:HQ¯fr→HQ¯fr obtained by taking direct sums of (5). The main result of the article is the following:

Theorem

(5.6) The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }: \mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}\rightarrow {\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}_{\tau }$$\end{document}ψ:HQ¯fr→HτQ¯fr is an injective morphism of graded algebras.

The subscript \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ in the notation refers to the fact that the algebra structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr is appropriately twisted, although in a simple and geometrically meaningful way. In words, the theorem above says that the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ realizes the cohomology of Nakajima varieties as a subalgebra of the framed CoHA \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}_{\tau }$$\end{document}HτQ¯fr, and on this subalgebra, CoHA’s multiplication is identified with taking stable envelopes.

As an interesting application of the previous theorem, we produce an explicit inductive formula for the stable envelopes of Nakajima varieties. The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ allows giving a tautological presentation of the stable envelopes by setting\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Stab}_{{\mathfrak {C}}}^{\varvec{\psi }}:=\frac{\varvec{\psi }\circ \text {Stab}}{e({\hbar }{\mathfrak {g}}_\text {v})}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}StabCψ:=ψ∘Stabe(ħgv).This map has as target a localization of the tautological ring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{T_\text {w}}([T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/G_\text {v}])$$\end{document}HTw([T∗Rep(v,w)/Gv]) and it restricts to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Stab}_{{\mathfrak {C}}}$$\end{document}StabC on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})\subset [T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/G_\text {v}]$$\end{document}M(v,w)⊂[T∗Rep(v,w)/Gv].

Applying Theorem 5.6, we show that for every fixed component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} By induction on the dimension of A, this formula gives an explicit presentation of the stable envelopes of Nakajima varieties for the action of a torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\subset A_\text {w}$$\end{document}A⊂Aw. This formula can be seen as the cohomological limit of the main result of [6], where an analogous formula is presented in the elliptic setting.

Further directions

This article can be seen as the starting point of a larger project aimed at establishing a connection between the geometric theory of quantum groups initiated with the introduction of stable envelopes and cohomological Hall algebras. We conclude this introduction with a brief discussion of the prospective directions of development of the author’s current work.

K-theory, elliptic cohomology and qKZB equations

First of all, the results of the present paper will be extended to K-theory and elliptic cohomology in the companion paper [5]. While the results in K-theory are very close to the cohomological ones and can be essentially guessed from this article, the elliptic setting requires a more sophisticated approach. This is partly due to features intrinsic to elliptic cohomology and partly because the elliptic stable envelopes depend on extra parameters called dynamical (or Kähler) parameters. In view of this, the algebras of this article will be replaced by certain algebra objects in an appropriate monoidal category, whose tensor product involves some shifts of the dynamical parameters. Because of that, this construction can be seen as a dynamical version of the sheafified elliptic cohomological Hall algebra of Yang and Zhao [40].

An important application of the CoHA’s structure of stable envelopes is in the world of difference equations. Integral solutions for the quantum Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equations (qKZ) associated with arbitrary quivers can be expressed as:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\gamma \subset G_\text {v}} \text {Stab}^K \Gamma _q, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫γ⊂GvStabKΓq,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _q$$\end{document}Γq is a product of q-gamma functions and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Stab}^K $$\end{document}StabK is a K-theoretic stable envelope. The proof of this result is due to Okounkov and his coauthors [2, 24, 27], and is based on the equivalence of certain enumerative counts in K-theory. As these methods cannot be easily transferred to elliptic cohomology, the problem of finding solutions for the quantum Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov–Bernard equations (qKZB), which are the elliptic incarnation of the qKZ equations, is still open for general quivers.

In a joint work of the author with Giovanni Felder and Keyu Wang, the CoHA’s algebras structure of the elliptic stable envelopes will be exploited to produce such solutions. This project widely generalizes the results of Felder, Tarasov, and Varchenko [11, 12], who had already obtained solutions for type-A quivers.

Cohomological Hall algebras and stable envelopes of bow varieties

The elliptic stable envelope of a Nakajima variety X can be interpreted as the transformation matrix that relates the vertex functions of X, which are certain enumerative invariants that provide q-holonomic modules for a wide class of difference equations such as the q-KZ equations [1], with the vertex functions of its 3d mirror dual \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^!$$\end{document}X!. Consequently, stable envelopes can be considered an adequate topological invariant to test 3D mirror symmetry. The first affirmative results were obtained in [31, 32], but only in the fortunate case when the 3D-dual of the Nakajima variety X is itself a Nakajima variety. Indeed, the class of Nakajima varieties is not closed under mirror symmetry, so to work in full generality, one needs to consider a larger class, namely bow varieties, which have been introduced in the context of enumerative geometry in [30]. In joint work with Richárd Rimányi [8], it is shown that all the main ideas of this paper, namely stable envelopes, cohomological Hall algebras, and their interplay, can be extended to bow varieties. Moreover, these results are exploited to prove fusion rules for R-matrices and 3D mirror symmetry of elliptic stable envelopes [7].

Preprojective CoHA and Yangians

Going back to the cohomological level, another version of CoHA can be related to those discussed in this article. Namely, one can consider the preprojective version of (1) by setting\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {P}}^{{\overline{Q}}}=\bigoplus _{\text {v}\in \mathbb {N}^I} {\mathcal {P}}^{\overline{Q}}(\text {v})\qquad \qquad {\mathcal {P}}^{\overline{Q}}(\text {v})=H^\text {{BM}}([\mu _{\text {v}}^{-1}(0)/G_\text {v}]), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PQ¯=⨁v∈NIPQ¯(v)PQ¯(v)=HBM([μv-1(0)/Gv]),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^\text {{BM}}$$\end{document}HBM stands for Borel–Moore homology. As shown in [41], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {P}}^{{\overline{Q}}}$$\end{document}PQ¯ acts on cohomology of Nakajima varieties by maps\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {P}}^{{\overline{Q}}}(\text {v}_1)\otimes _\Bbbk \mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}(\text {v}_2,\text {w})\rightarrow \mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}(\text {v}_1+\text {v}_2,\text {w}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PQ¯(v1)⊗kHQ¯fr(v2,w)→HQ¯fr(v1+v2,w).Davison in [9] and Schiffmann and Vasserot in [34] conjectured in that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {P}}^{{\overline{Q}}}$$\end{document}PQ¯ is isomorphic to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_\textrm{MO}^{Q,+}$$\end{document}YMOQ,+, the positive half of the Maulik–Okounkov Yangian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_\textrm{MO}^{Q}$$\end{document}YMOQ. This was first shown for the 1-loop quiver in [10, 19]. In parallel, Yang and Zhao [37, 40, 41] showed that an appropriate subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {P}}^{{\overline{Q}}}$$\end{document}PQ¯ is isomorphic to the positive half of Drinfel’d Yangians of type ADE.

More recently, Schiffmann and Vasserot [34, 35] proved half the conjecture by building an injective map from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {P}}^{\overline{Q}}\otimes _\Bbbk \text {Frac}(\Bbbk )$$\end{document}PQ¯⊗kFrac(k) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_\textrm{MO}^{Q,+} \otimes \text {Frac}(\Bbbk )$$\end{document}YMOQ,+⊗Frac(k), which intertwines the actions of these algebras on the cohomology of Nakajima varieties. The key technical step in their work consisted in identifying certain generators of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {P}}^{{\overline{Q}}}\otimes _\Bbbk \text {Frac}(\Bbbk )$$\end{document}PQ¯⊗kFrac(k), expressed in terms of Hecke correspondences acting on the cohomology of Nakajima varieties, with the matrix coefficients of the classical r-matrix of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_\textrm{MO}^{Q,+}$$\end{document}YMOQ,+, which in turn is generated by stable envelopes. In other words, Schliffman and Vasserot identify the appropriate pull–push diagrams of certain matrix coefficients of the equivariant residue of the stable envelope.

The main result of the present paper goes in a similar but independent direction by showing that the whole stable envelope comes from the pull–push diagram defining the multiplication map (2) of the framed CoHA \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}_{\tau }$$\end{document}HτQ¯fr.

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {P}}^{{\overline{Q}}}$$\end{document}PQ¯ admits a canonical algebra morphism to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{\overline{Q}}}\subset {\mathcal {H}}^{{\overline{Q}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯⊂HQ¯fr, we expect Schliffman–Vasserot’s result to be compatible with ours and hope that a positive answer to this prediction would allow shedding more light on the connection between the preprojective CoHA and Maulik–Okounkov’s Yangians \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_\textrm{MO}^{Q,+}$$\end{document}YMOQ,+.

Elliptic quantum groups

Of course, preprojective CoHAs can be also constructed in K-theory and elliptic cohomology [28, 40]. On the other hand, a definition of the elliptic quantum group of an arbitrary quiver is missing in the literature. As a consequence, an identification of the preprojective CoHA with the positive half of quantum groups in cohomology and K-theory could be used to define the elliptic quantum group itself as a Drinfel’d double of the elliptic preprojective CoHA.

Setup

Cohomology functors

Let G be an algebraic group. The cohomology functor that we consider in this article is equivariant cohomology\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_G(-): G\text {-Spaces}\rightarrow \text {Alg}_{H_G(*)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HG(-):G-Spaces→AlgHG(∗),taken with complex coefficients. Given a G-space X, its G-equivariant cohomology \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_G(X)$$\end{document}HG(X) is defined as the ordinary cohomology of the space\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X_G= (X\times EG)/G, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}XG=(X×EG)/G,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$EG\rightarrow BG$$\end{document}EG→BG is the classifying space of G. By construction, it is an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_G(*)=H(BG)$$\end{document}HG(∗)=H(BG)-algebra.

In this article, we only consider the case when G is either a torus T, a parabolic subgroup P of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GL}(n)$$\end{document}GL(n), or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GL}(n)$$\end{document}GL(n) itself. In these cases, EG and BG admit explicit representatives. The infinite Grassmannian\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Gr}({n},{\infty })=\lim _{k\rightarrow \infty } \text {Gr}({k},{\mathbb {C}^n}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gr(n,∞)=limk→∞Gr(k,Cn)is a model for the classifying space of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B\text {GL}(v)$$\end{document}BGL(v) with universal bundle given by its tautological bundle of rank n. Similarly, a model for the classifying space of a n-dimensional torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\cong (\mathbb {C}^\times )^n$$\end{document}T≅(C×)n is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathbb {P}^\infty )^n$$\end{document}(P∞)n, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}^\infty =\text {Gr}({1},{\infty })$$\end{document}P∞=Gr(1,∞), and the universal bundle ET is the direct sum of the tautological line bundles living on each copy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}^\infty $$\end{document}P∞. As a consequence, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{T}(*)= \mathbb {C}[{\mathfrak {t}}]\qquad H_{\text {GL}(n)}(*)=\mathbb {C}[{\mathfrak {t}}]^W, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HT(∗)=C[t]HGL(n)(∗)=C[t]W,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {t}}$$\end{document}t is the Lie algebra of T and W is the Weyl group of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GL}(n)$$\end{document}GL(n). Let now P be a parabolic subgroup of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GL}(n)$$\end{document}GL(n) containing the Levi subgroup \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\cong \prod _i GL(n_i)$$\end{document}L≅∏iGL(ni). Its classifying space fits in the Cartesian squaresAdditionally, notice that BP is homotopy equivalent to BL and a fibration over BGL(n) by partial flags \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GL}(n)/P$$\end{document}GL(n)/P.

Beyond the standard pullback in cohomology, there are also pushforward maps\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_*: H_G(X)\rightarrow H_G(Y) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f∗:HG(X)→HG(Y)associated with proper equivariant morphisms between smooth varieties \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f: X\rightarrow Y$$\end{document}f:X→Y. Their construction is standard and is obtained through Poincaré duality in Borel–Moore homology.

In the article, we will also consider the cohomology of quotients stacks [X/G], which is the algebraic counterpart of equivariant cohomology. As discussed in [3], the cohomology of [X/G] coincides with the equivariant cohomology of the prequotient:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H([X/G])=H_G(X). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H([X/G])=HG(X).More generally, if we are given an action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G\times H$$\end{document}G×H on X, we can define the H-equivariant cohomology of [X/G] as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{H}([X/G])=H([X/(G\times H)]). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HH([X/G])=H([X/(G×H)]).

Stacks of quiver representations

Let (Q, I) be a quiver, i.e., an oriented graph, with finite vertex set I, where edge-loops and multiple edges are allowed. The quiver data are encoded in the adjacency matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q=\lbrace Q_{ij} \rbrace $$\end{document}Q={Qij}, where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Q_{ij}=\lbrace \text {number of edges from} \; i \; \text {to} \; {j}\rbrace . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Qij={number of edges fromitoj}.By abuse of notation, we identify the set of oriented edges with the matrix Q. There are two natural “head" and “tail" maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h,t:Q\rightarrow I$$\end{document}h,t:Q→I that associate with an oriented edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in Q$$\end{document}e∈Q its head and tail vertices h(e) and t(e), respectively.

A representation of a quiver(Q, I) is an assignment of a vector space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_i$$\end{document}Vi to every vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in I$$\end{document}i∈I and a linear map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{h(e),t(e)}\in \text {Hom}(V_{t(e)},V_{h(e)})$$\end{document}xh(e),t(e)∈Hom(Vt(e),Vh(e)) to every edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in Q$$\end{document}e∈Q. The space of representations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Rep}(\text {v})$$\end{document}Rep(v) is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Rep}(\text {v})=\bigoplus _{e\in Q}\text {Hom}(V_{(t(e))}, V_{(h(e))}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Rep(v)=⨁e∈QHom(V(t(e)),V(h(e))).where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_i=\mathbb {C}^{\text {v}_i}$$\end{document}Vi=Cvi.

Given a quiver (Q, I), we also introduce the space of framed representations\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})\cong \text {Rep}(\text {v})\oplus \bigoplus _{i\in I}\text {Hom}(V_i, W_i) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Rep(v,w)≅Rep(v)⊕⨁i∈IHom(Vi,Wi)with dimension\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {v}_i=\dim (V_i)\qquad \text {w}_i=\dim (W_i), \qquad \text {v},\text {w}\in \mathbb {N}^I. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}vi=dim(Vi)wi=dim(Wi),v,w∈NI.Equivalently, this can be seen as the space of representations of the framed quiver \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( Q_{\text {fr}}, I_{\text {fr}})$$\end{document}(Qfr,Ifr), which is the quiver whose set of vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ I_{\text {fr}}$$\end{document}Ifr consists of two copies of I and whose adjacency matrix is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Q_\text {fr}=\begin{pmatrix} Q &{} \quad 0\\ 1 &{} \quad 0 \end{pmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Qfr=Q010.Also notice that the cotangent bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v,w) can be identified, via trace pairing, with the representations of the framed doubled quiver \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} {fr}})$$\end{document}(Qfr¯,I¯fr), defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \begin{document}$$\overline{Q}_\text {fr}=Q_\text {fr}+Q_\text {fr}^T$$\end{document}Q¯fr=Qfr+QfrT.

Finally, we remark that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Rep}(\text {v},0)$$\end{document}Rep(v,0) is naturally identified with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Rep}(\text {v})$$\end{document}Rep(v) and, similarly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},0)$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v,0) is identified with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v})$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v), the space of representations of the (unframed) doubled quiver \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\overline{Q},\overline{I})=(Q+Q^T, I)$$\end{document}(Q¯,I¯)=(Q+QT,I).

The groups\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{\text {v}}:=\prod _{i\in I}\text {GL}(\text {v}_i)\qquad G_{\text {w}}:=\prod _{i\in I}\text {GL}(\text {w}_i) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gv:=∏i∈IGL(vi)Gw:=∏i∈IGL(wi)act naturally on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v,w) by change of basis. There is also an action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}_{\hbar }^\times $$\end{document}Cħ× rescaling the cotangent directions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v,w), and we denote the corresponding weight by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hbar }$$\end{document}ħ. Overall, we get an action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {v}\times G_\text {w}\times \mathbb {C}^\times _{\hbar }$$\end{document}Gv×Gw×Cħ× on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v,w).

Notice that the vector space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v,w) admits a canonical symplectic structure, which is preserved by the action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {v}\times T_\text {w}$$\end{document}Gv×Tw and is shifted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}^\times _{\hbar }$$\end{document}Cħ× with weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hbar ^{-1}}$$\end{document}ħ-1.

Definition 2.1

The moduli space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {R}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}R(v,w) is the quotient stack \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/G_\text {v}]$$\end{document}[T∗Rep(v,w)/Gv].

Since we will never consider the stack \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/G_\text {v}]$$\end{document}[Rep(v,w)/Gv], no confusion should arise from the notation.

Notice that we only take the quotient with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {v}$$\end{document}Gv, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {R}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}R(v,w) admits a residual action by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {w}\times \mathbb {C}^\times _{\hbar }$$\end{document}Gw×Cħ×. We will be actually interested in the action of a maximal torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_\text {w}\subset G_\text {w}\times \mathbb {C}^\times _{\hbar }$$\end{document}Tw⊂Gw×Cħ×, which we factor as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_\text {w}=A_\text {w}\times \mathbb {C}^\times _{\hbar }$$\end{document}Tw=Aw×Cħ× where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_\text {w}=\prod _{i\in I} A_{\text {w}_i}$$\end{document}Aw=∏i∈IAwi is a maximal torus in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {w}$$\end{document}Gw.

Nakajima quiver varieties

By \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}_{Q,\theta }(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}MQ,θ(v,w), we denote the Nakajima variety associated with a quiver (Q, I), dimension vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {v},\text {w}\in \mathbb {N}^I$$\end{document}v,w∈NI and stability condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \mathbb {Z}^{I}\cong \text {Char}(G_\text {v})$$\end{document}θ∈ZI≅Char(Gv), cf. Namely, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}_{Q,\theta }(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}MQ,θ(v,w) is realized as the GIT-symplectic reduction of the space of representations A striking feature of Nakajima varieties is that the fixed components of the action of a subtorus of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_\text {w}$$\end{document}Aw are still quiver varieties [19, Section 2.4.1].

Proposition 2.2

Framed CoHA

The basic construction

Remark 3.1

The previous grading is equivalent to an action of a one-dimensional torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}^\times $$\end{document}C× acting on the various spaces with the weights prescribed by their degrees. However, we believe that the former is the right perspective, especially when it comes to studying the relation between stable envelopes and CoHAs.

Following Kontsevich and Soibelman [18], we define a multiplication map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\textsf{m}}:{\mathcal {H}}\otimes _{\Bbbk }{\mathcal {H}}\rightarrow {\mathcal {H}}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}m:H⊗kH→Hthat turns \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}$$\end{document}H into a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {N}^I\times \mathbb {N}^I$$\end{document}NI×NI-graded \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-algebra. Each graded component\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\textsf{m}}:{\mathcal {H}}(\text {v}_1,\text {w}_1)\otimes _{\Bbbk }{\mathcal {H}}(\text {v}_2,\text {w}_2)\rightarrow {\mathcal {H}}(\text {v}_1+\text {v}_2,\text {w}_1+\text {w}_2) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m:H(v1,w1)⊗kH(v2,w2)→H(v1+v2,w1+w2)is defined by means of correspondence (9). Theorem 3.2

The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}$$\end{document}m defines an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {N}^I\times \mathbb {N}^I$$\end{document}NI×NI-graded associative unital \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-algebra structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr with unit element given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\in \Bbbk ={\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}(0,0)$$\end{document}1∈k=HQ¯fr(0,0).

Definition 3.3

We call the associative unital \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {N}^I\times \mathbb {N}^I$$\end{document}NI×NI-graded algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}},{\textsf{m}})$$\end{document}(HQ¯fr,m) the framed doubled cohomological Hall algebra associated with the quiver (Q, I).

Notice that, seen as a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-module, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr does not depend on the edges of Q, encoded in the multiplication map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}$$\end{document}m nonetheless.

Remark 3.4

The (unframed) CoHA associated with the doubled quiver \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\overline{Q}, {\overline{I}})$$\end{document}(Q¯,I¯) defined by Kontsevich–Soibelman is recovered as the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {w}=0$$\end{document}w=0 subalgebra of our framed CoHA \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr. Indeed, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-submodule with trivial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {w}$$\end{document}w-grading is preserved by the multiplication, which reduces to the one defined in [18, Section 2.2]. On the other hand, the algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr should not be confused with Kontsevich–Soiblelman cohomological Hall algebra associated with the framed doubled quiver \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\overline{Q}}_{\text {fr}},{\overline{I}}_{\text {fr}})$$\end{document}(Q¯fr,I¯fr). Indeed, the graded component of the latter is identified with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*_{G_{\text {v}}\times G_{\text {w}}\times \mathbb {C}^\times _{\hbar }}(T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w}))$$\end{document}HGv×Gw×Cħ×∗(T∗Rep(v,w)) rather than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*_{G_{\text {v}}\times T_{\text {w}}}(T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w}))$$\end{document}HGv×Tw∗(T∗Rep(v,w)), and also the multiplication is affected by the change of group.

To relate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr with stable envelopes, we need to twist the multiplication\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\textsf{m}}:{\mathcal {H}}(\text {v}_1,\text {w}_1)\otimes _\Bbbk {\mathcal {H}}(\text {v}_2,\text {w}_2)\rightarrow {\mathcal {H}}(\text {v}_1+\text {v}_2,\text {w}_1+\text {w}_2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m:H(v1,w1)⊗kH(v2,w2)→H(v1+v2,w1+w2).This is done as follows: multiplication by the class\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}$$\end{document}m to get a twisted multiplication \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}_\tau $$\end{document}mτ. It is easy to see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}_\tau $$\end{document}mτ also defines unital associative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-algebra structure on the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr.
