Definition 3.5

We call the associative unital \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {N}^I\times \mathbb {N}^I$$\end{document}NI×NI-graded \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}_\tau , {\textsf{m}}_\tau )$$\end{document}(HτQ¯fr,mτ) the twisted framed doubled cohomological Hall algebra associated with the quiver (Q, I).

Remark 3.6

The introduction of the twist is not new in the literature. Indeed, Yang and Zhao introduced in [41] the same twist to define a canonical morphism between the CoHA associated with the preprojective path algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}{\overline{Q}}/J_{\mu }$$\end{document}CQ¯/Jμ and the twisted version of Kontsevich–Soibelman CoHA for the doubled quiver \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{Q}}$$\end{document}Q¯. Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_{\mu }$$\end{document}Jμ is the ideal associated with the moment map of the action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {v}$$\end{document}Gv on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v})$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v). Equivalently, via dimensional reduction [9, Appendix A], the twist in the multiplication map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_\tau $$\end{document}mτ can be also detected in the critical CoHA for the tripled quiver with a non-trivial potential [13, 29, 39].

We also remark that, following Yang–Zhao’s construction, one could define a framed version of their preprojective cohomological Hall algebra together with an algebra morphism to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}_\tau $$\end{document}HτQ¯fr.

Shuffle description for the product

The framed CoHA admits a very explicit description under the identifications\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {H}}(\text {v},\text {w})=\Bbbk [{\mathfrak {a}}_\text {w}\times {\mathfrak {s}}_{\text {v}}]^{W_{G_{\text {v}}}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(v,w)=k[aw×sv]WGv.An element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in {\mathcal {H}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}f∈H(v,w) is simply a polynomial f(a, s), symmetric in each set of Chern roots associated with the groups \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GL}(\text {v}_i)\subset G_{\text {v}}$$\end{document}GL(vi)⊂Gv.

Fix decompositions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {v}=\text {v}_1+\text {v}_2$$\end{document}v=v1+v2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {w}=\text {w}_1+\text {w}_2$$\end{document}w=w1+w2 and view both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}H(v,w) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}(\text {v}_1,\text {w}_1)\otimes _{\Bbbk }{\mathcal {H}}(\text {v}_2,\text {w}_2)$$\end{document}H(v1,w1)⊗kH(v2,w2) as subalgebras of\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Bbbk [a_\text {w}\times Theorem 3.7

The product \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}(f_1,f_2)$$\end{document}m(f1,f2) of two elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1(a_1,s_1)\in {\mathcal {H}}(\text by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Shufflee(T∗Rep(v,w)[-1])f1f2e(gv[-1]).Similarly, the twisted product \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} Notice that the kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e(T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})[-1])/e({\mathfrak {g}}_{\text {v}}[-1])$$\end{document}e(T∗Rep(v,w)[-1])/e(gv[-1]) of the multiplication map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}$$\end{document}m coincides with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e({\mathfrak {N}}[-1])$$\end{document}e(N[-1]), the Euler class of the negative part of the normal bundle defined in Sect. 3.1.4.

Proof

We decompose the correspondence (9) giving rise to the multiplication map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}$$\end{document}m as follows:All the maps are the obvious ones. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_1$$\end{document}p1 is induced by the projection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z\rightarrow T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v}_1,\text {w}_1)\times T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v}_2,\text {w}_2) $$\end{document}Z→T∗Rep(v1,w1)×T∗Rep(v2,w2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_2$$\end{document}p2 is an affine bundle, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{p}}^*=(p_2^*)^{-1}\circ p_1^*$$\end{document}p∗=(p2∗)-1∘p1∗ is just the identity map in the tautological presentation. The map i is induced by the inclusion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z\hookrightarrow T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}Z↪T∗Rep(v,w) so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_*$$\end{document}i∗ is multiplication by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e(T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})[-1])$$\end{document}e(T∗Rep(v,w)[-1]), the Euler class of its normal bundle. Finally, the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _*$$\end{document}π∗ is modeled by the map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\tilde{\pi }}:T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})_P\rightarrow T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})_{G_{\text {v}}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}π~:T∗Rep(v,w)P→T∗Rep(v,w)Gv,which is a fibration by partial flags \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\text {v}}/P$$\end{document}Gv/P, and hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Ker}(d{\tilde{\pi }})={\mathfrak {g}}_\text {v}[-1]$$\end{document}Ker(dπ~)=gv[-1]. Thus10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi _*(f)={\tilde{\pi }}_*(f)=\text {Shuffle}\left( \frac{f}{e({\mathfrak {g}}_\text {v}[-1])}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}π∗(f)=π~∗(f)=Shufflefe(gv[-1]).Altogether,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\textsf{m}}(f_1,f_2)= & {} {\textsf{q}}_*\circ {\textsf{p}}^*(f_1\otimes f_2)=\pi _*\circ i_*\circ (p_2^*)^{-1}\circ p_1^*(f_1\otimes f_2)\\ {}= & {} \text {Shuffle}\left( \frac{e(T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})[-1])f_1f_2}{e({\mathfrak {g}}_\text {v}[-1])}\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m(f1,f2)=q∗∘p∗(f1⊗f2)=π∗∘i∗∘(p2∗)-1∘p1∗(f1⊗f2)=Shufflee(T∗Rep(v,w)[-1])f1f2e(gv[-1]),as claimed.

Finally, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}_\tau $$\end{document}mτ is defined as the composition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}$$\end{document}m with cup product by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e({\hbar }{\mathfrak {g}}_{\text {v}}[1])$$\end{document}e(ħgv[1]), also its formula follows. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 3.8

In [38, Section 2.3], Yang and Zhao define a framed shuffle algebra similar to the one introduced in Theorem 3.7. The only significant difference from our construction is that in their work the torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_\text {w}$$\end{document}Aw is replaced with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\text {w}}\supseteq A_{\text {w}}$$\end{document}Gw⊇Aw. Consequently, in [38] an additional shuffle symmetrizing the equivariant parameters a associated with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_\text {w}$$\end{document}Aw is considered.

Almost at the same time, Konno introduced an elliptic (and dynamical) version of the shuffle product from Theorem 3.7 in the case of type-A quivers with one framing vertex [17]. This structure was then extended in [6] to arbitrary quivers and framings.

CoHA via abelian quotients

Choose a maximal torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\subset G_\text {v}$$\end{document}S⊂Gv with Lie algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {s}}$$\end{document}s. The multiplication map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}_{\text {ab},\tau }$$\end{document}mab,τ uniquely factors through the following commutative diagramDenote the dashed arrow by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsf{m}}_{\text {ab},\tau }^{{\mathfrak {b}}}$$\end{document}mab,τb. Cohomological stable envelopes

Chambers, attracting sets and polarization

Let X be a smooth quasi-projective symplectic variety with an action a torus T rescaling the symplectic form with weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hbar }^{-1}$$\end{document}ħ-1 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\subset \ker ({\hbar })$$\end{document}A⊂ker(ħ). In this work, we will be interested in the case when\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X=T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})////^\theta H \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X=T∗Rep(v,w)////θHwhere H is either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {v}$$\end{document}Gv or a maximal torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\subset G_\text {v}$$\end{document}S⊂Gv thereof.

Definition 4.1

([19]) A chamber \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}$$\end{document}C is a connected component of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\text {Cochar}(A)\otimes _\mathbb {Z}\mathbb {R})\setminus \Delta $$\end{document}(Cochar(A)⊗ZR)\Δ, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta $$\end{document}Δ is the hyperplane arrangement determined by the A-weights of the normal bundles of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^A$$\end{document}XA. One says that a weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi \in \text {Char}(A)$$\end{document}χ∈Char(A) is attracting (resp. repelling) with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}$$\end{document}C if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{t\rightarrow 0} \chi \circ \sigma (t)=0$$\end{document}limt→0χ∘σ(t)=0 (resp. if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{t\rightarrow 0} \chi \circ \sigma (t)=\infty $$\end{document}limt→0χ∘σ(t)=∞) for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in {\mathfrak {C}}$$\end{document}σ∈C. The trivial character is called the A-fixed character.

Restricted to some fixed component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F\subset X^A$$\end{document}F⊂XA, any A-equivariant K-theoretic class V of X decomposes according to the characters of A in attracting, A-fixed and repelling classes\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} { \left. \hspace{0.0pt}V \right| _{F} }={ \left. \hspace{0.0pt}V \right| _{F,+} }+{ \left. \hspace{0.0pt}V \right| _{F,0} }+{ \left. \hspace{0.0pt}V \right| _{F,-} } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VF=VF,++VF,0+VF,-with respect to a chosen chamber \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}$$\end{document}C. For instance, we have that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} { \left. \hspace{0.0pt}TX \right| _{F} }={ \left. \hspace{0.0pt}TX \right| _{F,+} }+TF+{ \left. \hspace{0.0pt}TX \right| _{F,-} }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TXF=TXF,++TF+TXF,-,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_F^+={ \left. \hspace{0.0pt}TX \right| _{F,+} }$$\end{document}NF+=TXF,+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_F^-={ \left. \hspace{0.0pt}TX \right| _{F,-} }$$\end{document}NF-=TXF,- are the attracting and repelling parts of the normal bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_F$$\end{document}NF to F in X.

If X is a Nakajima variety and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=A_\text {w}$$\end{document}A=Aw, these geometrically defined chambers coincide with the Lie-theoretic chambers. Indeed, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_\text {w}$$\end{document}Aw-weights of the normal bundles of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_\text {w}$$\end{document}Aw-fixed components of a Nakajima variety X are the roots of the group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GL}(\sum _j \text {w}_j)$$\end{document}GL(∑jwj), and hence, the hyperplane arrangement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta $$\end{document}Δ coincides with the one that defines the Lie-theoretic chambers of a maximal torus of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GL}(\sum _j \text {w}_j)$$\end{document}GL(∑jwj).

Let F be a fixed component in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{A}$$\end{document}XA. The attracting set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Att}_{\mathfrak {C}}({F})=\{x\in X | \lim _{t\rightarrow 0} \sigma (t)\cdot x\in F, \text {for some} \sigma \in {\mathfrak {C}}\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}AttC(F)={x∈X|limt→0σ(t)·x∈F,for someσ∈C}is well defined, i.e., independent of the choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in {\mathfrak {C}}$$\end{document}σ∈C, and it is also an affine bundle over F. This follows from the standard results in [4]. Notice in particular that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${ \left. \hspace{0.0pt}T(\text {Att}_{\mathfrak {C}}({F})) \right| _{F} }=N^+_F$$\end{document}T(AttC(F))F=NF+.

We also recall the following key definition:

Definition 4.2

The full attracting set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Att}^f_{{\mathfrak {C}}}(F)$$\end{document}AttCf(F) is the minimal closed subset of X containing F and closed under taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Att}_{\mathfrak {C}}({-})$$\end{document}AttC(-).

The choice of a chamber determines a partial ordering on the set of fixed components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^A$$\end{document}XA by taking the transitive closure of the relation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Att}_{\mathfrak {C}}({F})\cap F'\ne \emptyset \quad \Rightarrow \quad F\ge F'. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}AttC(F)∩F′≠∅⇒F≥F′.See [19, Section 3.2.3] for a proof of the fact that this is really a partial order.

Stable envelopes

We now recall Maulik–Okounkov’s definition of stable envelopes.

Since A acts trivially on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^A$$\end{document}XA, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_T(X^A)\cong H_{T/A}(X^A)\otimes H_A(*), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HT(XA)≅HT/A(XA)⊗HA(∗),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_A(*)$$\end{document}HA(∗) is the ring of polynomials in equivariant parameters in A. Although the isomorphism above is not canonical, different isomorphisms give the same filtration of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_T(X^A)$$\end{document}HT(XA) by the degree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\deg _A$$\end{document}degA in the equivariant parameters.

With this observation, we can recall the definition of cohomological stable envelope.

The next lemma is a key result for the application of stable envelopes in the geometric representation theory of Yangians, see [19], and it also lies at the heart of the interpretation of stable envelopes in terms of CoHAs that we shall give.

Lemma 4.4

([19, Section 3.6]) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}$$\end{document}C be a chamber for the action of A on X, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}'\subset {\mathfrak {C}}$$\end{document}C′⊂C be a face of some dimension. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A'$$\end{document}A′ be torus whose Lie algebra is the span of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}'$$\end{document}C′ in the Lie algebra of A and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}/{\mathfrak {C}}'$$\end{document}C/C′ be the projection of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}$$\end{document}C on the Lie algebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A/A'$$\end{document}A/A′. Then, the following diagramis commutative.

Stable envelopes of Nakajima varieties: R-matrices

Let now X be a Nakajima variety \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X={\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}X=M(v,w) equipped with the action of the torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=T_\text {w}$$\end{document}T=Tw. A decomposition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {w}=\text {w}_1+\text {w}_2\dots +\text {w}_k$$\end{document}w=w1+w2⋯+wk gives a homomorphism\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A=\lbrace (a_1,a_2,\dots ,a_k) \mid a_i\in \mathbb {C}^\times \rbrace \hookrightarrow A_\text {w}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A={(a1,a2,⋯,ak)∣ai∈C×}↪Aw.By iteration of Proposition 2.2, we get15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})^A)=\bigoplus _{\sum _{j=1}^k \text {v}_j=\text {v}} \bigotimes _{j=1}^k H_{T_{\text {w}_j}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v}_j,\text {w}_j)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HTw(M(v,w)A)=⨁∑j=1kvj=v⨂j=1kHTwj(M(vj,wj)).and hence the stable envelope is a collection of maps\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{T_{\text {w}_1}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v}_1,\text {w}_1))\otimes _\Bbbk \dots \otimes _\Bbbk H_{T_{\text {w}_k}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v}_k,\text {w}_k)) \rightarrow H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HTw1(M(v1,w1))⊗k⋯⊗kHTwk(M(vk,wk))→HTw(M(v,w)).For this torus action, there are k! possible chambers, namely\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathfrak {C}}_{\sigma }=\lbrace a_{\sigma (1)}<a_{\sigma (2)}<\dots <a_{\sigma (k)}\rbrace \qquad \sigma \in S_k. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Cσ={aσ(1)<aσ(2)<⋯<aσ(k)}σ∈Sk.The change of basis matrix relating stable envelopes for two different chambers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}_{\sigma }$$\end{document}Cσ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}_{\tau }$$\end{document}Cτ, namely the map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R_{{\mathfrak {C}}_{\tau }, {\mathfrak {C}}_{\sigma }}:=\text {Stab}_{{\mathfrak {C}}_{\tau }}^{-1}\circ \text {Stab}_{{\mathfrak {C}}_{\sigma }}\in H_{T_\text {w}}(X^A)\otimes \mathbb {C}(\text {Lie}(A)) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}RCτ,Cσ:=StabCτ-1∘StabCσ∈HTw(XA)⊗C(Lie(A))is called R-matrix and is a fundamental object in the geometric representation theory of Yangians developed by Maulik and Okounkov [19]. In particular, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=3$$\end{document}k=3 different factorizations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{{\mathfrak {C}}_{\tau }, {\mathfrak {C}}_{\sigma }}$$\end{document}RCτ,Cσ provide solutions of the classical Yang–Baxter equation with spectral parameters.

Stable envelopes of hypertoric varieties: explicit formulas

If we consider the GIT-symplectic reduction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v,w) by a maximal torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\subset G_\text {v}$$\end{document}S⊂Gv with stability condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \text {Char}(S)$$\end{document}θ∈Char(S)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {M}}_S(\text {v},\text {w}):=T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})////^\theta S=\mu _S^{-1}(0)^{\theta -ss}/S, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}MS(v,w):=T∗Rep(v,w)////θS=μS-1(0)θ-ss/S,we produce a hypertoric variety rather than a Nakajima quiver variety. Like quiver varieties, they come equipped with an action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_\text {w}$$\end{document}Tw and, for a generic stability condition, they are smooth symplectic varieties, so it makes sense to talk about their stable envelopes. Moreover, also for hypertoric varieties, Kirwan surjectivity holds [16], and hence,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}_S)\cong \Bbbk [{\mathfrak {a}}_\text {w}\times {\mathfrak {s}}]/I, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HTw(MS)≅k[aw×s]/I,where I is some ideal. For an explicit description of the generators of I, see [16].

Remarkably, stable envelopes of hypertoric varieties admit a very explicit description in terms of tautological classes. This description was originally developed in full generality by Shenfeld [36], but for our interests, it suffices to have an explicit formula for the stable envelope map of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Stab}_{\lbrace a< 0\rbrace }: H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}_{S_1}(\text {v}_1,\text {w}_1)\times {\mathcal {M}}_{S_2}(\text {v}_2,\text {w}_2))\rightarrow H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}_S(\text {v}_1+\text {v}_2,\text {w}_1+\text {w}_2)) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Stab{a<0}:HTw(MS1(v1,w1)×MS2(v2,w2))→HTw(MS(v1+v2,w1+w2))Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=S_1\times S_2$$\end{document}S=S1×S2 and the torus action considered is the one of the one-dimensional torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}^\times \subset A_\text {w}$$\end{document}C×⊂Aw acting with weight one on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_1$$\end{document}W1 and trivially elsewhere. For this one-dimensional torus action, the chamber arrangement is particularly easy. Indeed, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Cochar}(\mathbb {C}^\times )\otimes _\mathbb {Z}\mathbb {R}=\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}Cochar(C×)⊗ZR=R, so are only two chambers: the positive axis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lbrace a>0 \rbrace $$\end{document}{a>0} and the negative axis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lbrace a<0 \rbrace $$\end{document}{a<0}.

Notice that the cohomology16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}_{S_1}(\text {v}_1,\text {w}_1)\times {\mathcal {M}}_{S_2}(\text {v}_2,\text {w}_2)) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HTw(MS1(v1,w1)×MS2(v2,w2))of the fixed component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}_{S_1}(\text {v}_1,\text {w}_1)\times {\mathcal {M}}_{S_2}(\text {v}_2,\text {w}_2)\subset {\mathcal {M}}_{S}(\text {v}_1+\text {v}_2,\text {w}_1+\text {w}_2)^{\mathbb {C}^\times }$$\end{document}MS1(v1,w1)×MS2(v2,w2)⊂MS(v1+v2,w1+w2)C× is isomorphic to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Proposition 4.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_\gamma \in \Bbbk [{\mathfrak {a}}_\text {w}\times {\mathfrak {s}}] $$\end{document}pγ∈k[aw×s] be a polynomial representing a class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}_{S_1}(\text {v}_1,\text {w}_1)\times {\mathcal {M}}_{S_2}(\text {v}_2,\text {w}_2))$$\end{document}γ∈HTw(MS1(v1,w1)×MS2(v2,w2)). Then, the polynomial\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e(T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})[-1])p_\gamma \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e(T∗Rep(v,w)[-1])pγrepresents the class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Stab}_{\lbrace a< 0\rbrace }(\gamma )$$\end{document}Stab{a<0}(γ).

For the proof, see [36, Section 4.3]. The fact that this assignment gives a well-defined map can be also seen as follows: by equivariant formality, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Since this map satisfies the axioms of a stable envelope (see [36]), by uniqueness of the latter, it is actually independent of the choice of the basis and of its polynomial representatives.

Abelianization of stable envelopes

In this section, we recall the abelianization of stable envelopes, which is a procedure that relates the stable envelopes of a Nakajima variety\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})=T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})////G_\text {v}=\mu _{\text {v},\text {w}}^{-1}(0)^{G_\text {v}-ss}/G_{\text {v}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(v,w)=T∗Rep(v,w)////Gv=μv,w-1(0)Gv-ss/Gvwith the ones of the abelian quotient\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {M}}_S(\text {v},\text {w}):=T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})////S=\mu _S^{-1}(0)^{S-ss}/S. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}MS(v,w):=T∗Rep(v,w)////S=μS-1(0)S-ss/S.In the following, we assume that the stability condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \text {Char}(G_\text {v})\subset \text {Char}(S)$$\end{document}θ∈Char(Gv)⊂Char(S), which determines the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta , G_\text {v})$$\end{document}(θ,Gv)-semistable locus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta , S)$$\end{document}(θ,S)-semistable locus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} respectively, is chosen generically, in such a way that both the varieties above are smooth. Abelianization of stable envelopes was first developed by Shenfeld [36] for singular cohomology and then reproduced in elliptic cohomology by Aganagic and Okounkov in [1]. The following version is the cohomological limit of abelianization in elliptic cohomology. Let B be a Borel subgroup of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {v}$$\end{document}Gv containing S, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {s}}\subset {\mathfrak {b}}\subset {\mathfrak {g}}_{\text {v}}$$\end{document}s⊂b⊂gv be the corresponding Lie algebras. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {s}}^*$$\end{document}s∗, which we identify with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} We can now review the statement of the abelianization theorem.

Theorem 4.6

([1, Section 4.3]) The composition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Stab}^{}_{\mathfrak {C}}(F_S)\circ (j^A_-)_*$$\end{document}StabC(FS)∘(j-A)∗ factors through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(j_-)_*$$\end{document}(j-)∗, i.e., there exists a map making the diagramcommute. Then the following diagram also commutes

Since each of the maps in the composition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\pi ^A)_*\circ ((\Pi ^A)^*)^{-1}\circ (j^A_+)^* $$\end{document}(πA)∗∘((ΠA)∗)-1∘(j+A)∗ is surjective, the previous theorem expresses the stable envelopes of the Nakajima variety \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}M(v,w) in terms of those of the hypertoric variety \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}_S(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}MS(v,w).

Remark 4.7

Actually, in the original reference [1] Theorem 4.6 is presented in a slightly different way. Indeed, the composition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} Similarly, also the composition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((\Pi ^A)^*)^{-1}\circ (j^A_+)^*$$\end{document}((ΠA)∗)-1∘(j+A)∗ is replaced with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\tilde{j}}_-^A)^*$$\end{document}(j~-A)∗. Despite appearances, it is easy to see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\tilde{j}}_+)^*=(\Pi ^*)^{-1}\circ (j_+)^*$$\end{document}(j~+)∗=(Π∗)-1∘(j+)∗ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((\Pi ^A)^*)^{-1}\circ (j^A_+)^*=({\tilde{j}}_-^A)^*$$\end{document}((ΠA)∗)-1∘(j+A)∗=(j~-A)∗ under the identification (18). Indeed, the cohomology of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu _{\text {v},\text {w}}^{-1}(0)^{G_{\text {v}}-ss}/B$$\end{document}μv,w-1(0)Gv-ss/B is generated by the Chern roots of the tautological bundles, and both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\tilde{j}}_+)^*$$\end{document}(j~+)∗ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Pi ^*)^{-1}\circ (j_+)^*$$\end{document}(Π∗)-1∘(j+)∗ send Chern roots to Chern roots. Hence, they are the same map. This argument proves the first equality; the proof of the second one is similar. Therefore, the statement of the theorem above differs from the original one [1] only in the notation. However, in this article, we avoid using the maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{j}}_+$$\end{document}j~+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{j}}^A_+$$\end{document}j~+A because they are not algebraic.

Framed semistable CoHA via stable envelopes

The framed semistable preprojective CoHA

Fix a quiver Q and consider the graded \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-module\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}:=\bigoplus _{\text {v},\text {w}\in \mathbb {N}^I} \mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}(\text {v},\text {w}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr:=⨁v,w∈NIHQ¯fr(v,w),where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}(\text {v},\text {w}):=H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}_Q(\text {v},\text {w})). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr(v,w):=HTw(MQ(v,w)).As usual, we will drop the reference to the quiver whenever this is clear from the context.

We now define a graded multiplication on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}$$\end{document}H via stable envelopes\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Stab}_{\lbrace a_{1}<a_2 \rbrace }: \mathbb {H}(\text {v}_1,\text {w}_1)\otimes _\Bbbk \mathbb {H}(\text {v}_2,\text {w}_2)\rightarrow \mathbb {H}(\text {v}_1+\text {v}_2,\text {w}_1+\text {w}_2) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Stab{a1<a2}:H(v1,w1)⊗kH(v2,w2)→H(v1+v2,w1+w2)

Theorem 5.1

The previous multiplication map defines an associative unital \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {N}^I\times \mathbb {N}^I$$\end{document}NI×NI-graded \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-algebra structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}$$\end{document}H with unit given by the element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\in \Bbbk =\mathbb {H}_{0,0}$$\end{document}1∈k=H0,0.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {v}=\text {v}_1+\text {v}_2+\text {v}_3$$\end{document}v=v1+v2+v3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {w}=\text {w}_1+\text {w}_2+\text {w}_3$$\end{document}w=w1+w2+w3 and consider the stable envelope map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Stab}_{\lbrace a_1<a_2<a_3 \rbrace }: \mathbb {H}(\text \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Stab{a1<a2<a3}:H(v1,w1)⊗kH(v2,w2)⊗kH(v3,w3)→H(v,w).Applying Lemma 4.4 twice with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {C}}=\lbrace { a_1<a_2<a_3 \rbrace }$$\end{document}C={a1<a2<a3} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \rbrace }$$\end{document}{a1<a2} or to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lbrace { a_2<a_3 \rbrace }$$\end{document}{a2<a3}, we get exactly the commutative diagramthat proves the claim. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Definition 5.2

We call the associative unital \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {N}^I\times \mathbb {N}^I$$\end{document}NI×NI-graded \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}, \text {Stab})$$\end{document}(HQ¯fr,Stab) the framed semistable preprojective cohomological Hall algebra of Q.

Remark 5.3

Unlike in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}$$\end{document}H, some graded components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}_{\text {v},\text {w}}$$\end{document}Hv,w of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}$$\end{document}H are zero. This is a consequence of the fact that, because of the stability condition, some Nakajima varieties \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}M(v,w) are empty. However, the Nakajima variety \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}(0,0)$$\end{document}M(0,0) is a singleton for every quiver Q and stability condition, and hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}(0,0)$$\end{document}H(0,0) is always equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k.

Remark 5.4

One could naively try to define a CoHA structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}$$\end{document}HQ¯fr by means of a correspondence of the formwhich is the restriction of (8) to the appropriate zero loci and semistable loci. However, it is easy to check that the dashed map is generally not well defined because it does not respect semistability. Substituting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Z\cap \mu _{\text {v},\text {w}}^{-1}(0))^{ss}$$\end{document}(Z∩μv,w-1(0))ss with the open subset for which the dashed map is well defined does not solve the problem because then the inclusion inside \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\mu _{\text {v},\text {w}}^{-1}(0))^{ss}$$\end{document}(μv,w-1(0))ss is not proper anymore, and hence, it does not descend to a pushforward in cohomology. Stable envelopes can be interpreted as a refined correspondence, obtained by iterated corrections on lower strata, that guarantees both the existence of a dashed map and the properness of the solid arrow (the corrections come with multiplicities, so, in general, the variety \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Z\cap \mu _{\text {v},\text {w}}^{-1}(0))^{ss}$$\end{document}(Z∩μv,w-1(0))ss is replaced with a Borel–Moore cycle, i.e., a collection of varieties with multiplicities). As a consequence, one can look at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Stab}_{{\mathfrak {C}}}$$\end{document}StabC as the natural substitute of the correspondence giving rise to a CoHA structure on the cohomology of Nakajima varieties.

Framed CoHA versus framed semistable preprojective CoHA

In this section, we review Aganagic–Okounkov’s construction of a map4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{\psi }: \mathbb {H}(\text {v},\text {w})\rightarrow {\mathcal {H}}(\text {v},\text {w}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ:H(v,w)→H(v,w)that is, up to a multiplicative factor, a section of the restriction map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{j}^*:H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathfrak {R}}(\text {v},\text {w}))\twoheadrightarrow H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}j∗:HTw(R(v,w))↠HTw(M(v,w))associated with the inclusion\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{j}:{\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})= \mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}}(0)^{G_{\text {v}}-ss}/G_{\text {v}}\hookrightarrow [T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/ G_{\text {v}}] = {\mathfrak {R}}(\text {v},\text {w}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}j:M(v,w)=μv,w-1(0)Gv-ss/Gv↪[T∗Rep(v,w)/Gv]=R(v,w).Like the stable envelopes, the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ solves an extension problem, which goes under the name of non-abelian stable envelope [26]. One can think of the ordinary “abelian” stable envelopes519\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Stab}^{}_{\mathfrak {C}}(F): H_{T_\text {w}}(F)\rightarrow H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}StabC(F):HTw(F)→HTw(M(v,w))as a map extending a topological class defined on some attracting locus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Att}_{\mathfrak {C}}({F})\subset {\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}AttC(F)⊂M(v,w) to a class on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}M(v,w) supported on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Att}_{{\mathfrak {C}}}^{f}(F)$$\end{document}AttCf(F). This extension is obtained by iteration, progressively adding attracting sets sprouting up from the boundary. Analogously, the non-abelian stable envelope \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ extends a topological class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})= \mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}}(0)^{G_{\text {v}}-ss}/G_{\text {v}}$$\end{document}M(v,w)=μv,w-1(0)Gv-ss/Gv to a class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }(\alpha )$$\end{document}ψ(α) on the ambient stack \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {R}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}R(v,w). As for the ordinary stable envelope, the non-abelian extension problem is characterized by a support condition, namely by requiring the class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }(\alpha )$$\end{document}ψ(α) to be supported on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}}(0)/G_{\text {v}}]\subset {\mathfrak {R}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}[μv,w-1(0)/Gv]⊂R(v,w). The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ can be built inductively using a stratification of the unstable locus of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}}(0)$$\end{document}μv,w-1(0) [26]. This makes the analogy with the abelian stable envelope even more transparent since the strata of the unstable locus can be described in terms of attracting sets for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {v}$$\end{document}Gv-action.

As shown by Aganagic and Okounkov in [2], non-abelian stable envelopes of Nakajima varieties can be reduced to the abelian case. This approach slightly hides the geometric intuition behind the abovementioned extension problem but allows the known tools developed for ordinary stable envelopes to transfer to this non-abelian setting. For this reason, in this article, we focus on Aganagic–Okounkov’s approach6 to non-abelian stable envelopes.

The key idea behind the construction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ is to enlarge the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}}(0)^{G_{\text {v}}-ss}$$\end{document}μv,w-1(0)Gv-ss in such a way that it hosts a copy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v,w). To this end, consider the space of representations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w}+\text {v})$$\end{document}T∗Rep(v,w+v), which we visualize vertex-wise as20Notice that the framing extension produces an extra action of a copy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {v}$$\end{document}Gv on the framing, which we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {v}'$$\end{document}Gv′. Define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}+\text {v}}(0)^{iso}\subset \mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}+\text {v}}(0) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μv,w+v-1(0)iso⊂μv,w+v-1(0)as the open subset of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}+\text {v}}(0)$$\end{document}μv,w+v-1(0) determined by the requirement that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in I $$\end{document}i∈I either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _i$$\end{document}ϕi or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _i$$\end{document}ψi is an isomorphism, in such a way that we have a factorization\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}+\text {v}}(0)^{iso}\subset \mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}+\text {v}}(0)^{G_{\text {v}}-ss}\subset \mu ^{-1}_{\text {v},\text {w}+\text {v}}(0). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μv,w+v-1(0)iso⊂μv,w+v-1(0)Gv-ss⊂μv,w+v-1(0).For simplicity, we assume from now on that the stability condition is such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _i$$\end{document}ϕi is an isomorphism for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in I$$\end{document}i∈I. It is straightforward how to modify the construction below to deal with the most general case.
