Putting it all together, we get the sought-after non-abelian stable envelope map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{\psi }: H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w}))\rightarrow H_{T_\text {w}\times G_{\text {v}}'}({\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w}+\text {v}))= H_{T_\text {w}}([{\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w}+\text {v})/G_{\text {v}}'])\xrightarrow {\varvec{\iota }^*} H_{T_\text {w}}({\mathfrak {R}}(\text {v},\text {w})). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ:HTw(M(v,w))→HTw×Gv′(M(v,w+v))=HTw([M(v,w+v)/Gv′])→ι∗HTw(R(v,w)).The support condition of (22) implies the following:

Lemma 5.5

([2, Proposition 1,3]) The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ is supported on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\mu _{\text {v},\text {w}}^{-1}(0)/G_{\text {v}}]\subset [T^*\text {Rep}(\text {v},\text {w})/G_{\text {v}}]$$\end{document}[μv,w-1(0)/Gv]⊂[T∗Rep(v,w)/Gv]. Moreover, the restriction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{j}^*(\varvec{\psi }(\alpha ))$$\end{document}j∗(ψ(α)) is divisible by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e({\hbar }{\mathfrak {g}}_{\text {v}})$$\end{document}e(ħgv) and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha = \varvec{j}^*(\varvec{\psi }(\alpha ))/e({\hbar }{\mathfrak {g}}_{\text {v}}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α=j∗(ψ(α))/e(ħgv).

We can now state the main theorem of this article.

Theorem 5.6

The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }: \mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}\rightarrow {\mathcal {H}}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}_{\tau }$$\end{document}ψ:HQ¯fr→HτQ¯fr is an injective morphism of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {N}^I\times \mathbb {N}^I$$\end{document}NI×NI-graded \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Bbbk $$\end{document}k-algebras.

In words, the theorem above says that the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ realizes the equivariant cohomology of Nakajima varieties as a subalgebra of the framed CoHA, and on this subalgebra CoHA’s multiplication is identified with taking stable envelopes.

Remark 5.7

The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ is not compatible with the cup products \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha ,\beta )\mapsto \alpha \beta $$\end{document}(α,β)↦αβ on its domain and codomain. This can be easily seen as follows. By contradiction, fix a non-trivial graded component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}^{{{\overline{Q}}}_{\text {fr}}}(\text {v},\text {w})$$\end{document}HQ¯fr(v,w) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {v}\ne 0$$\end{document}v≠0 and assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }(\alpha \beta )=\varvec{\psi }(\alpha )\varvec{\psi }(\beta )$$\end{document}ψ(αβ)=ψ(α)ψ(β). Composing with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{j}^*$$\end{document}j∗, which is clearly compatible with the cup product, and applying Lemma 5.5, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e({\hbar }{\mathfrak {g}}_{\text {v}}) \alpha \beta = \varvec{j}^*\varvec{\psi }(\alpha \beta )= \varvec{j}^* (\varvec{\psi }(\alpha )\varvec{\psi }(\beta ))=\varvec{j}^*\varvec{\psi }(\alpha )\varvec{j}^*\varvec{\psi }(\beta )=e({\hbar }{\mathfrak {g}}_{\text {v}})^2 \alpha \beta \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e(ħgv)αβ=j∗ψ(αβ)=j∗(ψ(α)ψ(β))=j∗ψ(α)j∗ψ(β)=e(ħgv)2αβfor all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β. This equation would imply \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e({\hbar }{\mathfrak {g}}_{\text {v}})^2=e({\hbar }{\mathfrak {g}}_{\text {v}})$$\end{document}e(ħgv)2=e(ħgv), which is a contradiction since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e({\hbar }{\mathfrak {g}}_{\text {v}})\ne 0$$\end{document}e(ħgv)≠0.

Proof of Theorem 5.6: Step one

First of all, notice that injectivity follows at once from Lemma 5.5, so it remains to prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\psi }$$\end{document}ψ is a morphism of graded algebras. The proof can be conceptually divided into two parts. In the first part, we prove an “abelianized” version of the statement and in the second we apply Theorems 3.10 and 4.6 to reduce the claim to its abelianized version.

Proof of Theorem 5.6: Step two

We can now complete the proof by combining abelianization of stable envelopes and abelianization of CoHA. Lemma 5.10

Consider the following diagram of modules:Assume that all the faces commute except the outermost and that the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ is injective. Then also the outermost face commute.

Proof

By injectivity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ, it suffices to check that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \circ k\circ f =\delta \circ h\circ g$$\end{document}δ∘k∘f=δ∘h∘g. Indeed, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \delta \circ h\circ g= {\hat{h}} \circ \beta \circ g={\hat{h}}\circ {\hat{g}}\circ \alpha ={\hat{k}}\circ {\hat{f}}\circ \alpha ={\hat{k}} \circ \gamma \circ f= \delta \circ k\circ f. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δ∘h∘g=h^∘β∘g=h^∘g^∘α=k^∘f^∘α=k^∘γ∘f=δ∘k∘f.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Applying now the second parts of Theorems 3.10 and 4.6, we get the solid diagram25while the dashed arrows are those constructed in the previous section. Commutativity of the outermost face is our final goal. The top left oblique map is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\pi _{1}\times \pi _{2})_*\circ ((\Pi _1\times \Pi _2)^*)^{-1}\circ (j_{1,+}\times j_{2,+})^*$$\end{document}(π1×π2)∗∘((Π1×Π2)∗)-1∘(j1,+×j2,+)∗, and the top right oblique map is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(q_{1}\times q_{2})_*\circ ((Q_1\times Q_2)^*)^{-1}\circ (i_{1,+}\times i_{2,+})^*$$\end{document}(q1×q2)∗∘((Q1×Q2)∗)-1∘(i1,+×i2,+)∗. By construction, the two lateral sides and the back of the diagram commute. Moreover, the oblique arrows are surjective, so to complete the proof it suffices to show that the two remaining faces, the top and the bottom of the cube, are commutative. Therefore, the following lemma concludes the proof.

Lemma 5.11

The top and bottom faces of the cubic diagram above commute.

Proof

As for the previous lemma, the top face is just two copies of the bottom face tensored together, so it suffices to prove the commutativity of the bottom face. Recall that the dashed arrow in the bottom face is built from diagram (24), on which we now build up the following commutative diagramPassing to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\times T_\text {w}$$\end{document}S×Tw-equivariant cohomology, we get the commutative diagramTaking quotients by B and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\text {v}$$\end{document}Gv, we can enlarge the previous commutative diagram as followsNotice that the right side of the square consists of the maps appearing in the lower right side of diagram (25). Similarly, the maps on the left side of the square are the ones appearing in the diagram As before, this diagram is the second abelianization diagram for the fixed component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})={\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w})\times {\mathcal {M}}(0,\text {v})\subset {\mathcal {M}}(\text {v},\text {w}+\text {v})$$\end{document}M(v,w)=M(v,w)×M(0,v)⊂M(v,w+v), see Theorem 4.6. Combining the last diagram with the outer frame of the penultimate one, we get the following commutative diagramThe two unlabeled horizontal maps are simply the canonical change of group maps. The commuting outer frame of this diagram was exactly what we had to prove that commutes. Therefore we are done. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Theorem 5.12
